## Document 065: 2026年9月宏观数据分析：出口再提速、物价回升，内需依然偏弱

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年9月宏观数据分析：出口再提速、物价回升，内需依然偏弱.pdf
Report source: 西南期货
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
研究员：万亮 邮箱：xnqh_wl@swfutures.com
期货从业证书号：F03116714 交易咨询从业证书号：Z0019298
时间：2026.10.20
出口再提速、物价回升，内需依然偏弱
——2026 年 9 月宏观数据分析

### Page 2

2
我们梳理了 2026 年 9 月国内重要的宏观经济数据，以增加对当前宏观经
济运行节奏的理解。
总的来说，2026 年 9 月国内经济延续了进出口强、内需弱；生产强、需求
弱的格局。三季度国内生产总值同比增长 4.5%，比二季度回升 0.2 个百分点，
前三季度同比增长 4.6%。9 月制造业 PMI 重回扩张区间，进出口增速进一步
加快，CPI 和 PPI 同比涨幅双双扩大，社零增速小幅回升，固定资产投资和房
地产销售延续下行趋势，实体经济融资需求和信心偏弱。
对此，我们认为，需要理性客观的看待当前宏观经济 K 型分化形势。新经
济保持高景气度，但尚不足以对冲房地产和基建等传统经济的下行，国内经济
的复苏不能一蹴而就。当前外部不稳定不确定因素较多，国内有效需求不足，
经济运行面临不少挑战。这要求实施更加积极有为的宏观政策，持续扩大内需、
优化供给，推动经济实现质的有效提升和量的合理增长。当前房地产市场处在
企稳复苏的关键节点，后续对宏观经济的拖累有望显著收窄。整体而言，2026
年面临的宏观环境好于 2025 年，但强劲复苏的可能性不大。

一、制造业 PMI 重回扩张区间
9 月份，制造业采购经理指数（PMI）为 50.1%，比上月上升 0.3 个百分点，
升至扩张区间。从企业规模看，大型企业 PMI 为 50.6%，与上月持平，高于临
界点；中、小型企业 PMI 分别为 49.7%和 48.9%，比上月上升 0.3 个和 1.0 个
百分点，均低于临界点。
从分类指数看，构成制造业 PMI 的 5 个分类指数中，生产指数、新订单指
数和供应商配送时间指数高于临界点，原材料库存指数和从业人员指数低于临

### Page 3

3
界点。生产指数为 51.7%，比上月上升 1.3 个百分点，表明制造业生产活动扩
张加快。新订单指数为 50.5%，比上月下降 0.1 个百分点，表明制造业市场需
求继续改善。原材料库存指数为 48.2%，比上月上升 0.1 个百分点，表明制造
业主要原材料库存量降幅略有收窄。从业人员指数为 48.4%，比上月下降 0.3
个百分点，表明制造业企业用工景气水平回落。供应商配送时间指数为 50.1%，
与上月持平，表明制造业原材料供应商交货时间继续加快。
9 月份，非制造业商务活动指数为 50.2%，比上月上升 1.2 个百分点，非制
造业景气水平明显回升。分行业看，建筑业商务活动指数为 50.3%，比上月上
升 3.4 个百分点；服务业商务活动指数为 50.2%，比上月上升 0.9 个百分点。
总的来说，9 月制造业 PMI 环比回升 0.3 个百分点，升至临界点以上，制
造业景气水平有所改善，但中、小型企业仍处于收缩区间。
二、9 月 CPI 同比上涨 0.9%，PPI 同比上涨 5.0%

2026 年 9 月份，全国居民消费价格同比上涨 0.9%。其中，城市上涨 0.9%，
农村上涨 0.8%；食品价格下降 1.7%，非食品价格上涨 1.5%；消费品价格上涨
图 1：中国制造业 PMI
（数据来源：ifind 资讯）

1.0%，服务价格上涨 0.8%。
9 月份，全国居民消费价格环比上涨 0.2%。其中，城市上涨 0.2%，农村上
涨 0.4%；食品价格上涨 0.4%，非食品价格上涨 0.2%；消费品价格上涨 0.5%，
服务价格下降 0.2%。
9 月份，食品烟酒及在外餐饮类价格同比下降 0.9%，影响 CPI 下降约 0.26
个百分点。食品中，鲜菜价格下降 8.1%，影响 CPI 下降约 0.14 个百分点；畜
肉类价格下降 3.7%，影响 CPI 下降约 0.15 个百分点，其中猪肉价格下降 10.0%；
鲜果价格下降 1.5%，影响 CPI 下降约 0.03 个百分点；蛋类价格上涨 14.3%，
影响 CPI 上涨约 0.07 个百分点。其他七大类价格同比六涨一降。其中，其他用
品及服务、交通通信、医疗保健价格分别上涨 5.9%、4.3%和 2.7%，教育文化
娱乐、衣着、生活用品及服务价格分别上涨 1.4%、1.2%和 0.2%；居住价格下
降 0.3%。
2026 年 9 月份，全国工业生产者出厂价格同比上涨 5.0%，环比上涨 1.2%。

工业生产者购进价格同比上涨 6.9%，环比上涨 1.2%。其中，煤炭开采和洗选
业、有色金属冶炼和压延加工业价格同比分别上涨 35.3%和 21.4%，石油和天

### Page 5

5
然气开采业、石油煤炭及其他燃料加工业、化学原料和化学制品制造业价格同
比分别上涨 24.5%、21.6%和 12.1%，对 PPI 形成较大拉动；计算机通信和其他
电子设备制造业、电气机械和器材制造业价格同比分别上涨 5.8%和 6.2%，AI
投资的高景气度仍在带动电子、计算机和通信行业的价格回升。
总的来说，2026 年物价指数的回升，主要受输入性通胀拉动。9 月 CPI 和
PPI 同比涨幅双双扩大，其中交通工具用能源价格同比涨幅由 8.3%扩大至
14.9%，是 CPI 回升的主要拉动。CPI 涨幅仍明显滞后于 PPI，验证了国内产能
充足、内需相对不足的局面。9 月 PPI 环比上涨 1.2%、同比涨幅扩大 1.2 个百
分点，主要受国际大宗商品价格上行传导和国内产业转型升级带动部分行业需
求增加影响。
三、进出口延续高增
以美元计，9 月，中国进出口总值 7608.4 亿美元，同比增长 34.1%；出口
总值 4471.8 亿美元，同比增长 36.3%；进口总值 3136.5 亿美元，同比增长 31.0%；

贸易顺差 1335.3 亿美元。
图 3：中国 PPI 同比增速与环比增速
（数据来源：ifind 资讯）

### Page 6

6
其中，9 月，集成电路出口额同比增长 136%（前值为 129.8%）、自动数
据处理设备及其零部件出口额同比增长 92.9%（前值为 76.5%）。国内进出口
均受益于全球 AI 投资的高景气度。
分国别来看，9 月中国对美出口 478.81 亿美元，同比增速为 39.6%；对欧
盟的出口额为 601.51 亿美元，出口增速为 25.3%；对东盟国家的出口额为 817.04
亿美元，同比增长 52.3%；对日本的出口金额为 156.23 亿美元，同比增速为
15.8%。对主要贸易伙伴出口均保持两位数正增长。

### Page 7

7
得益于中国产业强大竞争力和旺盛的海外需求，出口增速进一步走高，特
别是 AI 投资强相关的计算机、电子和通信行业进出口均保持高增。展望未来，
中国外贸真正的风险不在于美国关税的增加，而在于美国经济衰退风险加大，
全球经济增速放缓导致的需求下降。预计 2026 年中国进出口均有望延续高景
气态势。
四、实体经济信贷需求偏弱
初步统计，2026 年 9 月末社会融资规模存量为 468.17 万亿元，同比增长
7.1%。其中，对实体经济发放的人民币贷款余额 279.9 万亿元，同比增长 4.8%；
对实体经济发放的外币贷款折合人民币余额 1.25 万亿元，同比增长 6.4%；委
托贷款余额 11.34 万亿元，同比增长 1.5%；信托贷款余额 4.53 万亿元，同比增
长 0.7%；未贴现的银行承兑汇票余额 2.22 万亿元，同比下降 8.8%；企业债券
余额 36.62 万亿元，同比增长 9.3%；政府债券余额 105.23 万亿元，同比增长
13.7%；非金融企业境内股票余额 12.73 万亿元，同比增长 5.8%。

初步统计，2026 年前三季度社会融资规模增量累计为 27.46 万亿元，比上
年同期少 2.63 万亿元。其中，对实体经济发放的人民币贷款增加 11.5 万亿元，
同比少增 3.04 万亿元；对实体经济发放的外币贷款折合人民币增加 2451 亿元，
同比多增 3397 亿元；委托贷款增加 222 亿元，同比多增 794 亿元；信托贷款
减少 1383 亿元，同比多减 3387 亿元；未贴现的银行承兑汇票增加 748 亿元，
同比少增 2263 亿元；企业债券净融资 2.75 万亿元，同比多 1.18 万亿元；政府
债券净融资 10.3 万亿元，同比少 1.16 万亿元；非金融企业境内股票融资 5359
亿元，同比多 2191 亿元。

### Page 8

8
居民信贷结构上，9 月居民贷款基本零增长，同比少增约 3890 亿元；其
中，短期贷款减少 900 亿元，同比少增约 2300 亿元；中长期贷款增加 874 亿
元，同比少增约 1626 亿元。居民短期与中长期贷款新增仍处低位，显示居民
消费和购房信贷需求偏弱。
企业信贷结构上，9 月企业贷款增加 1.29 万亿元，同比多增 700 亿元。其
中，短期贷款增加 4600 亿元，同比少增 2500 亿元；中长期贷款增加 8900 亿
元，同比少增 200 亿元；票据融资减少 500 亿元，同比多增约 3526 亿元。企
业短期与中长期贷款新增仍处在低位，表明企业信心和预期偏弱，企业融资需
求不强。
1-9 月政府债券净融资 10.3 万亿元，同比少 1.16 万亿元。财政扩张对社融

回落 0.3 个百分点。狭义货币（M1）余额 116.76 万亿元，同比增长 3.2%，增

速环比回落 0.9 个百分点。M1-M2 剪刀差回落至-4.0%。

总的来说，2026 年 9 月金融数据依旧偏弱，居民和企业部分信贷需求不

均不高。
五、社零增速小幅回升，固定资产投资延续下行
9 月份，规模以上工业增加值同比实际增长 5.5%。从环比看，9 月份，规
模以上工业增加值比上月增长 0.61%。1-9 月份，规模以上工业增加值同比增
长 5.3%。
1-9 月份，社会消费品零售总额 370001 亿元，同比增长 1.1%。其中，除汽
车以外的消费品零售额 340574 亿元，增长 2.8%。9 月份，社会消费品零售总
额 42433 亿元，同比增长 1.1%。其中，除汽车以外的消费品零售额 38792 亿
元，增长 3.9%。
按经营单位所在地分，9 月份，城镇消费品零售额 36077 亿元，同比增长
0.8%；乡村消费品零售额 6356 亿元，增长 2.7%。按消费类型分，9 月份，商
品零售额 37830 亿元，同比增长 1.0%；餐饮收入 4603 亿元，增长 2.1%。

图 7：中国 M1 和 M2 增速

平方米，下降 25.1%。其中，住宅竣工面积 16301 万平方米，下降 26.6%。
9 月末，商品房待售面积 74825 万平方米，同比下降 1.5%。其中，待售
3 年以下面积 54197 万平方米，下降 5.0%。狭义的商品房待售面积库存继续
下降，库存持续去化。
2026 年 9 月房地产销售面积、新开工和投资继续回落。我们仍然认为，房
图 11：中国商品房施工和竣工增速
（数据来源：ifind 资讯）
图 12：中国商品房待售面积

### Page 13

13
地产市场的企稳复苏是一个漫长的过程，难以一蹴而就。当前，房地产市场已
处于底部阶段，房地产新开工面积、投资等或已处于长期均衡水平下。随着基
数的下降，后续商品房销售面积、销售额同比增速降幅将逐步收窄。随着市场
预期的转变，本轮房地产下行周期的“市场底”正在出现。
尽管存在巨大的分歧，2026 年有望成为房地产市场止跌回稳的关键节点。
积极因素在逐步累积，房地产对宏观经济和资本市场的拖累将显著收窄。
七、总结与展望
总的来说，2026 年 9 月国内经济延续了进出口强、内需弱；生产强、需求
弱的格局。三季度国内生产总值同比增长 4.5%，比二季度回升 0.2 个百分点，
前三季度同比增长 4.6%。9 月制造业 PMI 重回扩张区间，进出口增速进一步
加快，CPI 和 PPI 同比涨幅双双扩大，社零增速小幅回升，固定资产投资和房
地产销售延续下行趋势，实体经济融资需求和信心偏弱。
对此，我们认为，当前外部不稳定不确定因素较多，国内有效需求不足，
经济运行面临不少挑战。这要求实施更加积极有为的宏观政策，持续扩大内需、

优化供给，推动经济实现质的有效提升和量的合理增长。当前房地产市场处在
企稳复苏的关键节点，后续对宏观经济的拖累有望显著收窄。整体而言，2026
年面临的宏观环境好于 2025 年，但国内经济强劲复苏的可能性不大。
跟踪后续政策的落地细节，观察物价端的回升力度，耐心等待宏观经济的
向上信号。

## Document 091: 261009_daiwa_umc

Source PDF: all report pdf/261009_daiwa_umc.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: Given its longstanding position in the global foundry industry, 
ever challenging TSMC’s leadership during the late 1990s, we believe 
UMC stands well to benefit from a ripple effect in demand spurred by AI 
scaling, as detailed in the main section of this report. We expect UMC’s 
wafer loadings to reach 100% from 1Q27, with price hikes to go through 
(see Price hike to go through? Still likely, 31 July). That said, we believe the 
equity market has priced in this AI strength on extra volume and pricing 
power for UMC. Hence, we stick with our Hold (3) rating on the counter.

What’s the impact: 3Q26 preview and 4Q26 outlook. We expect UMC to 
report 3Q26 net profit of TWD16.4bn (+9% YoY), varying not much from the 
Bloomberg consensus. Its monthly revenue finished at c.TWD49bn QTD, 
reaching 65% of our/consensus 3Q26 estimates, which we consider 
tracking inline. For 4Q26, we expect UMC to earn net profit of TWD16bn, 
13% below consensus, as the street seems to be assuming higher non-
operating gains. UMC is scheduled to report results on 28 October; we will 
be offering updates in due course.

<=28nm ramp on AI scaling and fab optimisation. UMC’s 28/22nm-and-
below revenue contribution has hovered around the high-30% range over 
the past 2 years, after meaningful ramp during the COVID period on 
broadened demand applications from display drivers and TV SoCs to 
power management. With AI scaling creating a ripple effect in companion 
chip demand for connectivity (ie, silicon photonics) and power (ie, server 
PMICs), we believe UMC is well positioned to benefit from any spillover 
demand, with the strength augmented by TSMC’s fab optimisation, which 
could strengthen UMC’s pricing power. Given that UMC’s wafer loadings 
are likely to reach 100% from 1Q27, we expect its price hikes to go through 
and model increases of 2-5%, effective from 1Q27, across nodes related to 
AI applications. This should resume its <=28nm ramp to exceed a half of 
revenue in 2028. All in, we raise our 2027-28E EPS by 22%.

What we recommend: Despite our forecast revisions, we remain 
comfortable with our Hold (3) rating for UMC, as we believe its AI strength 
is already priced in. We raise our 12M TP to TWD145 (previous: TWD108), 
based on our higher ROE-adjusted PBR target of 4.2x (previous: 3.3x) 
applied to 1-year-forward BVPS on a higher ROE trajectory. Upside/ 
downside risks would be any price hikes above our expectations/ 
geopolitical overhangs (war, FX, interest rate) weighing on end-demand.

How we differ: Our 2026-28E EPS do not vary much from consensus, 
indicating that UMC’s AI strength is already reflected in its valuation.

9 October 2026

United Microelectronics

A beneficiary of the AI ripple effect, but …

➢ UMC looks set to benefit from AI demand spillover 
➢ Yet we believe the AI ripple effect is being priced in 
➢ Maintain our Hold (3) call, with a higher 12M TP of TWD145

Company profile 
Incorporated in Taiwan, United Microelectronics Corp (UMC) is one of the leading semiconductor 
foundries in the world and second largest in Taiwan. UMC offers a series of foundry services from 
photo-mask, wafer fabrication to wafer probe & test. Its wafer fabs are spread over Taiwan, China and 
Singapore, with technology offerings ranging from 0.25u and above, 0.18/0.15u, 0.13u, 90nm, 65nm, 
45/40nm, 28/22nm to 14nm FF.

United Microelectronics (2303 TT): 9 October 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 8 Oct 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company 
ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
UMC’s Board of Directors consists of nine members whose expertise covers industry, academia,
finance, accounting, and management. Among these directors, six are independent directors,
representing 67% of total directors in 2024. The percentage of independent directors is high
relative to the regulatory requirement of at least 20% or at least 2 independent directors.
Although some of UMC's executive management are also directors, UMC's Chairman and
President are not the same person. Considering this and its relative-high percentage of
independent directors, we believe the board can perform its functionalities well
Capital management 2 It kept its dividend payout ratios above 50% in 2017-25, which is at a healthy, sustainable level.Overall, we see the balance between investment and dividend as appropriate.
Related party &
transaction 1 Sales to related parties were around 1-2% of its total revenue each year. The percentage isinsignificant and thus we see limited risk for UMC.
S Supply chainmanagement 2
UMC has constructed an evaluation framework for reviewing the sustainable development of its
supply chains based on UMC’s GG-026-004-C sustainable supply-chain management review
guidelines to ensure its suppliers fulfil environmental protection, social responsibility, labour and
human rights, safety & health and sustainable development guidelines. We believe these efforts
could reduce its supply-chain risk.
E Water & wastewatermanagement 2
UMC developed its “Water Management Policy and Commitment” in 2015 to promote more
effective utilisation of water resources. In line with this policy, UMC continues to enhance
recycling through increased use of reclaimed and desalinated water, ongoing wastewater
treatment R&D, and optimised discharge management. In 2024, the company achieved a 30.3%
reduction in water withdrawal per unit product, surpassing its 14% goal and meeting its 2030
target ahead of schedule, which demonstrates UMC’s strong commitment to water conservation.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
2
To achieve the goal of zero pollution, UMC has established a comprehensive new material
evaluation process to effectively screen and control new materials introduced into the
development of new processes. Through this effort, it can avoid or reduce the use of raw
materials and the associated environmental impact. UMC was the first semiconductor
manufacturer company in the world to obtain QC-080000 IECQ Hazardous Substance Process
Management (HSPM) certification for all its fabs by leading global semiconductor manufacturers
in completing the third-party verification and audit of its HSPM system in 2006.
E GHG emissions 2
UMC was the first semiconductor company in Taiwan to pass integrated environmental
protection, safety and health management system certifications in 2001. UMC has established
the UMC Low Carbon Commitment as a guide for future carbon reduction projects. Its GHG
emission reduction goals include: 1) to reduce fluorinated greenhouse gas emissions intensity by
65% over 2013-25 vs. 2010, and 2) to reduce electricity consumption intensity by 15% in 10
years over 2016-25 vs. 2015.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

Greater China Technology Hardware  | Asia Pacific

Racks in September 2026;
Introduce 2027 Rack Forecast
We provide monthly/quarterly rack shipment forecasts for
ODMs. Within the major Server ODMs, our order of preference
is Wistron  > Hon Hai > Quanta, based on upside to PT.
Morgan Stanley Taiwan Limited+
Howard Kao

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
We introduce 2027 forecast at 90.4K racks (+10% y/y) vs our 2026 forecast now
at 82K racks: This year is a strong one for downstream rack assemblers, with rack

shipments up ~209% y/y, but we expect rack shipments to grow another ~10% y/y
to reach 90.4K in CY27. For CY27, our forecast implies Hon Hai will be a share gainer
with rack share up to 43% (from 37% in CY26), with Quanta staying unchanged at
26% and Wistron's share up slightly to 24% (from 23% in CY26).
Quanta (2382.TW, OW): September revenue was NT$325.3B (-23% m/m, +77% y/y).
We attribute the m/m decline mainly to lower GB200/300 rack shipments at 1.55-

1.60K racks (-38% m/m), despite being offset slightly by higher NB shipments at
4.2M units (+62% m/m, -9% y/y). In 3Q, Quanta shipped ~6.2K racks (+3% q/q),
which came in slightly lower than our forecast of ~6.4K racks. Total 3Q revenue
reached ~NT$1,116M (+8% q/q, +125% y/y), which is 15% above MSe but in-line with
the Street. For 2026, we forecast Quanta to ship 20.9K racks, and we introduce our
preliminary 2027 forecast at 23.5K (+12% y/y).
Wistron (3231.TW, OW): September revenue was  NT$380.8B (-17% m/m, +87% y/

y). We attribute the m/m decline to: 1) lower GB200/300 server computing tray
shipments  (1.25-1.30K rack equivalents, by our estimates, down approximately 46%
m/m), despite higher Wiwynn revenue at ~NT$172.6B (+20% m/m, +100% y/y) and
higher NB shipments at 1.5M units (+7% m/m, -32% y/y), flat DT shipments at 500K
(flat m/m, -44% y/y), and higher monitor shipments at 950K (+27% m/m, +6% y/y).
In 3Q, Wistron shipped ~4.8K racks (+22% q/q), which came in slightly lower than

our forecast of ~5.1K racks. Total 3Q revenue reached ~NT$1,149M (+28% q/q,
+102% y/y), which is 10% above MSe and 9% above the Street. For 2026, we
forecast Wistron to ship 19.13K racks, and we introduce our preliminary 2027
forecast at 22K (+15% y/y).
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with
companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result,
investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of
interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.
Key Takeaways
We estimate GB200/300 rack output in September  at ~6.5K (-23% m/m).
Quanta shipped 1.55-1.60K  GB200/GB300 racks.
Wistron shipped 1.25-1.30K GB200/GB300 racks.
Hon Hai shipped ~2.7K GB200/300 racks.
We also introduce our 2027 forecast at 90.4K racks (+10% y/y).

Hon Hai (2317.TW, OW): September revenue was NT$1,158.6B (+26% m/m, +38% y/

components and other products, cloud and networking products, and lastly
computing. We model HH's GB200/300 rack shipments at 2.7K (+4% m/m). This
brings total 3Q rack shipments at 7.8K racks (+1% q/q). For 2026, we forecast
Foxconn to ship 30.3K racks, and we introduce our preliminary 2027 forecast at

38.8K (+28% y/y).

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Hon Hai Precision (2317.TW)
Base case scenario value, derived from a residual income valuation model. Our key assump-
tions include a cost of equity of 8.5%, a medium-term growth rate of 13%, and a terminal
growth rate of 3%.
Risks to Upside
n Better-than-expected iPhone sell-through
n Faster progress in AI server business
n Faster EV business development progress
n Any new M&A activity that could improve sentiment

Risks to Downside
n Lower iPhone sell-through
n Smaller profit contribution from AI business
n Slower EV business development progress
n Geopolitical developments that could negatively affect foreign investment
Quanta Computer Inc. (2382.TW)
Base case, residual income model. Key assumptions include a cost of equity of 9.0% (beta of
1.2, equity premium of 6.0% and risk-free rate of 1.5%), an 8.5% medium-term growth rate, and
a 3% terminal growth rate.

Risks to Upside
n Stronger-than-expected NB demand
n Stronger-than-expected Apple Watch demand
n Stronger-than-expected server demand
n Faster-than-expected AI server penetration
Risks to Downside
n Weaker-than-expected NB demand
n Softer-than-expected Apple Watch demand
n Weak margin performance owing to rising labor costs and sales shortfalls
n Fierce price competition in the mega data center segment
n Slower-than-expected AI server penetration

Wistron Corporation (3231.TW)
Base case, residual income valuation. Key assumptions: 8.7% cost of equity, 7.0% medium-
term growth rate and 3% terminal growth rate.
Risks to Upside
n Faster-than-expected divestiture of consumer electronics business
n Stronger-than-expected NB demand
n Margin expansion from better product mix

## Document 307: EcoNews of 12 October 2026 (2026-10-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 12 October 2026 (2026-10-12).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
5
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.37 / 12 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Stronger trade posture pays off. The EU and China have agreed to
restrain hybrid cars exports to the EU to a level half or less than cur -
rently projected over the next four years, cutting exports by “several
million units” though still allowing them to rise. China also agreed to

drop its tariffs on EUR 4 bn of EU exports and provide easier access to
Chinese rare earths and magnets under a fast-track licensing sche -
me. These announcements were made at the conclusion of EU Trade
Commissioner Maroš Šefcovic’s visit to Beijing, following a recent es -
calation in trade tensions. Meanwhile, France and Germany released
a joint non-paper on Strengthening European resilience, addressing
global macroeconomic imbalances and market distorting practices

calling notably for:
i) Broadening the tools the European Commission may use to address
unfair trade practices (including undervalued exchange rates) or im -
port surges;
ii) Procedural changes to make them easier to use (qualified majority
of member states needed to block rather than authorise them). These
measures will be discussed during the next EU council on October
15-16th.
The European Commission also unveiled a new critical materials pac -

kage involving 46 strategic extraction, processing or recycling projects
spread across 16 member states (including 5 in France). The goal is to
reduce the EU’s dependence on China for critical metals.
EUROZONE
Price pressures are broadening, activity holds up. Producer price in -
flation accelerated further in August (8.3% y/y 1 from 5.8%), driven by
energy. Pressures are becoming more widespread, notably in capital
goods and above all intermediate goods (6.8%). They are partly offset

by non-durable consumer goods, which remain in deflation (-0.8%).
Given the PPI's close correlation with the HICP , this points to further
upside risks to consumer inflation. On the activity front, final September
PMIs confirmed the flash estimates, with the composite index still solid
at 53.1 and the manufacturing index stable at 52.7. The construction
PMI improved but remains depressed (43.4; +0.4 pt). Retail sales edged
up 0.1% m/m2 in August.

France
Modest spread relief as budget positioning starts. The spread between
French and German 10Y yields narrowed to 136 bps 3 on October 9
(mostly thanks to a decline in the French yield). As the Finance Com -
mittee of the National Assembly started discussing the 2027 budget,
the government reiterated its strong intent not to deviate from the
5% of GDP deficit target and to get a budget adopted in time, likely
without a vote. This is the most likely outcome — but not to be im -

plemented before 10 December (given mandatory timelines) — which
effectively removes any incentive for MPs to back unpopular measures
during the process. We can therefore expect heated debates (including
multiple no-confidence votes being tabled but rejected) and a lack in
fiscal discipline. In parallel, the "Rassemblement National" party ("Na-
tional Rally" in English) presented its fiscal strategy, which includes an
alternative budget for 2027 and an aggressive fiscal consolidation path

throughout 2032. The party called for a referendum to constitutionalise
1 Year-on-year
2 Month-on-month
3 Basis points
a “golden rule” that would require a deficit at least 0.5 pp below the
debt-stabilising threshold (roughly 3% of GDP), as long as the public
debt ratio exceeds 60% of GDP . The golden rule would require a budget
balance adjustment of 0.5 pp per year if the fiscal deficit is more than
1 pp above the stabilising threshold. The adjustment measures put

forward lack specifics but likely overstate savings, understate costs
and rely on overly optimistic growth assumptions (despite large fiscal
consolidation, at least 1.7% annually over the next three years — a
pace reached only 4 times in France since 2008, apart from post-pan-
demic reopening, and well in excess of the roughly 1% growth potential
estimated by most international institutions). That said, it is significant
that this party has chosen to fly the flag for fiscal discipline loud and

clear. Other parties should do the same.
Activity is holding up. New vehicle registrations rose 11% y/y in Sep -
tember, confirming a marked acceleration over the past three months
(+9.4% y/y in Q3). In Q3, growth came entirely from electric vehicles
(+112% y/y), whose share of registrations reached an all-time high
(38% in Q3, 41% in September). Export growth continued in August,
both in services (+5.2% y/y YTD) and in goods (+5.6% y/y YTD) — though

driven by prices rather than volume in the latter case. Manufacturing
output posted only a moderate rebound in August (+0.3% m/m; fol -
lowing cumulative declines of 2.8% over the previous three months),
limited by renewed supply constraints in Q3 (which limited produc -
tion at 31.5% of companies; +5 pp compared to H1). Industrial produc-
tion fell (-0.3% m/m) as a result of a decline in electricity production
(-2.4% m/m) driven by the heatwave. Credit growth remains dynamic.

Bank and market financing to NFCs saw steady growth in August (+4%).
New housing loans rebounded slightly in August (EUR 12.2 bn) fol -
lowing a relatively weak July, whilst interest rates continued to rise.
Consumer credit remained buoyant.
Upcoming: Banque de France monthly Business Survey (Monday), Bu-
siness creations in September (Friday).
Germany
Bullish official growth forecast upgrade despite mixed data. The
German government raised its 2026 GDP growth forecast to 1.3%

— the fastest pace since 2017 — from a prior estimate of 0.5%, citing
stronger-than-expected exports and a surge in government spending.
The 2027 forecast was also raised to 1.1% from 0.9%. Industrial pro -
duction was stronger than expected in August (+2.3% y/y versus -1.7%
in July; the highest level since March 2025 and higher than antici -
pated), driven mainly by strong growth in construction output, prima -
rily in public works (+10.5%; to the highest level since November 2021).

By contrast, manufacturing output fell by -0.2% (after +3.3% in July)
due to a decline in transport equipment production (lowest level since
April 2022). Likewise, new factory orders hit their lowest level in one
year in August (-10.6% m/m). However, this decline is mainly due
to a base effect. Export growth remains solid: +4.4% YTD, despite a
-0.8% m/m decline in August. Exports to the Eurozone continue to rise
(+6.3% y/y YTD), while exports to the U.S. have returned to positive

territory (+1.1%) and those to China continue to decline (-12.9%). The
trade surplus reached EUR 19.5 bn in August (+4% y/y YTD, despite a
-9.8% m/m decline).

### Page 2

6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.37 / 12 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
ECONEWS Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
UNITED STATES
Mild slowdown in services and more rate hikes signaled. The non-ma-
nufacturing ISM survey fell 0.5 pp4 to 54.9 in September due to a sharp
slowdown in the business activity component (down 5.2 pp to 56.5 pp,
still a robust expansion). Employment rose and supply pressures in -
tensified for the first time since June, while the price gauge reached

its highest level (74, up 1.4 pp) since 2022. Meanwhile, the minutes
of the September FOMC meeting confirmed the Fed’s hawkish outlook
and noted that the AI-buildout carried growing weight in the reading of
current inflation data and outlook. Governor Waller and St. Louis Fed
President Musalem mentioned the need for additional rate hikes while
remaining flexible on timing. The New York Fed survey of consumer in-
flation expectations showed them rising to 3.9%. We continue to expect

two more hikes, the next one coming in December.
Upcoming: September CPI (Wednesday), September PPI and Retail
Sales (Thursday), September Industrial Production (Friday).
JAPAN
Solid wage growth fails to lift household spending. Total wage growth
eased to 3.8% y/y in August (down 0.5 pp) as bonus payments stalled
(0% y/y, after 5.3% y/y in July). This allowed continued real wage growth
at 1.5% y/y, extending an uninterrupted run of positive readings in 2026

as administrative measures kept inflation in check. However, this did
not prevent real household spending from contracting in August for the
ninth consecutive month (-3.1% y/y).
EMERGING MARKETS
The IIF reports that nonresident portfolio flows to EMs turned negative
at -USD 26.3 bn in September, after USD 11.4 bn in August, the first net
outflow since June, as a hawkish Fed lifted US real yields and the USD.
Equity outflows reached -USD 19.2 bn, led by heavy foreign selling of

Korean equities, while debt flows turned negative at -USD 7.0 bn as
hard currency demand faded and EM credit spreads widened late in
the month. Further hikes from DM central banks will create additional
headwinds, but EM should remain resilient as long as global growth
persists.
ASIA
China
Yuan policy defence. Coinciding with the Beijing visit of the EU trade
commissioner, the People's Bank of China issued an unusually lengthy
formal statement rejecting claims that the yuan is undervalued and

defending its exchange-rate management by reiterating that it has a
‘floating exchange rate regime’ with reference to a basket of currencies.
It further argued that China's trade surpluses are driven by rising in -
dustrial competitiveness rather than currency under-devaluation and
that exchange-rate adjustments will not resolve structural global im -
balances. Nonetheless, it allowed the yuan to hit a four-year high vs.
the CFETS basket — bringing its year-to-date gain to 5.7%. The PBOC

set the USD/CNY's reference rate at 6.7330, the strongest level since
2023.
India
Monetary tightening. The RBI raised its repo rate by 25bp to 5.5%, its
first hike since February 2023, and shifted to a “calibrated tightening”
stance amidst rising inflationary pressures. The RBI introduced new
measures to stabilise the rupee, including direct dollar supplies to
state-owned oil companies and tighter restrictions on FX derivatives.
4 Percentage points

EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Central Europe
Cautious monetary policy stance amid mild inflation uptick. Poland’s
and Romania’s Central banks kept policy rates unchanged at 3.75% and
6.5%, respectively. Inflation accelerated in September, with a mild up-
tick in the Czech Republic and Hungary (respectively 2.5% and 1.6% y/y)
and a more entrenched increase in Poland (4% y/y). Inflation is now
above the official target in the Czech Rep. and Poland, but fuel price

caps in both countries should contain inflation dynamics.
Gulf
PMIs for September point for a firm but uneven non-oil recovery. Both
Saudi and UAE PMIs stood at 55.3, driven mostly by export orders in
the UAE and by domestic demand in Saudi Arabia. On the contrary,
Qatar’s PMI fell again (to 46.7), while Kuwait’s PMI also declined but
remained in expansionary zone (at 52.4).
Türkiye
Manufacturing production contracted by 1.6% m/m in August, the lar-

gest monthly decrease since the beginning of the year. On a y/y ba -
sis, it fell by 2.3%. Both domestic demand and exports seem to have
contributed negatively to this poor outcome. On a positive note, in
September, CPI inflation (1.9% m/m, 27.9% y/y) slowed more than the
market expected, and exports rebounded.
LATIN AMERICA
Inflationary pressures slightly up across the region. In Brazil, headline
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.6% y/y from 4.2% y/y in August (above the

central bank's 4.5% target), partly reflecting the reversal of a tempora-
ry electricity price discount in August. In Mexico, inflation rose to 3.5%
in September from 3.3% in August, while in Colombia it reached 6.3%,
slightly up from 6.2% in August. Inflation remained high in Chile but
was stable at 4.1%. In Peru, it was also slightly up (4.2% vs. 4.1%); the
central bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 4.25% for the thirteenth
consecutive month but maintained an upward bias amid rising inflation

and energy prices.
COMMODITIES
Energy: Limited relief. Continuation of skirmishes in the Gulf of Hormuz
and escalation of the conflict between the Houthis and a Saudi-led
coalition left oil prices fluctuating around $100/b for Brent. However,
the complex security situation has been driving the cost of shipping to
record high levels and the price of physical barrel to early April levels.
Late on Friday, President Trump announced an agreement with Russian

President Putin to release to global markets 4.8 million tonnes of die -
sel (roughly 8 days of European consumption), provided conditions in
refineries allow for it, of which 300,000 tonnes immediately. The White
House also announced a suspension of sanctions on Russia’s exports
of diesel until April 2027. Energy experts expect a de minimis price
impact.
European gas prices (TTF) rose by 11.5% over the week to 82 €/MWh.
EU gas inventories reached 73.6% of full capacities on October 11 th

(the official target is 80% at end October).
Food inflation rose sharply in September. The FAO’s Food Price Index
grew by 5.8% y/y, up from just 3% in August. This was driven by cereal
(+17% y/y) and sugar prices (+15%).

## Document 379: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors

Source PDF: all report pdf/Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Thematic Reports)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 18
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

WS Global CIO Office

InvesTips

Roots and routes –  
A framework for Global 
Indian investors

September 2026

### Page 2

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  2

India’s financial coming of age has created a generation of investors at a 
distinctive crossroads: deeply connected to one of the world’s fastest -growing 
economies, yet increasingly aware that portfolios concentrated in a single market 
can accumulate risks over time. 
This report discusses the challenges faced by two distinct ‘Global Indian’ investor 
profiles. The first is the onshore Indian investor: a resident Indian whose wealth 
is largely denominated in domestic equities and fixed income, but who may still treat 
global markets as an occasional satellite allocation rather than a core portfolio 
component. The second is the offshore Indian investor : a non -resident Indian 
(NRI) based outside India with full access to global capital markets, yet who may 
be under-allocated to the growth story unfolding in their country of origin. Because 
global benchmarks understate India’s economic importance, investors seeking 
more meaningful participation in its growth story may consider allocating above 
benchmark levels, with NRIs’ potential stronger  affinity with India providing an 
additional reason to make this adjustment. 
For both groups, the starting point is the same: a concentrated bias towards either 
Indian or global markets can create risks that diversification can help mitigate 
without requiring investors to abandon their core convictions. For onshore Indian 
investors, global assets can provide structural currency diversification and access 
to sectors that are under -represented in Indian indices. Our Capital Market 
Assumptions (CMAs)  illustrate the potential portfolio benefits: increasing  
the allocation to global assets from 0% to 15% raises expected returns while 
reducing expected volatility meaningfully. As a result, the portfolio’s risk -adjusted 
returns improve. 
For offshore Indian investors, India offers a real yield premium, a rising weight in global indices and long-term growth that remains 
meaningfully under -represented in global benchmarks. A historical perspective illustrates the opportunity: since 2001,  
Indian equities have delivered over 10% annualised returns in USD terms, outperforming Developed Market equities over the 
same period. 
Onshore and offshore Indian investors may share the same connection to India, but they face very different portfolio challenges. 
There are key dividing lines that determine how each investor should think about diversification, risk management and the rol e 
of Indian assets within a broader investment portfolio.

Manpreet Gill 
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA 
Sylvain Huard 
Head of Asset Allocation 
Hannah Chew 
Portfolio Strategist

1 Two investor profiles, one shared 
opportunity

### Page 3

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  3

Base currency –  
the first and most 
important divide 
 The onshore Indian investor earns, spends, saves and ultimately retires in rupees (INR). Their 
relevant return is, therefore, an INR return. If the INR depreciates against the US dollar (USD), 
foreign assets rise in INR terms and can act as a natural hedge . If the INR appreciates, those 
same assets deliver lower INR returns than their local -currency performance would suggest. 
For the offshore investor, the currency problem is reversed. Their benchmark is typically USD-
based, and Indian markets are the exposure that introduces exchange-rate risk. A strong year 
for Indian equities in INR terms may look less compelling once translated back into USD at an 
unfavourable exchange rate.

Regulatory access –  
different constraints, 
different routes 
 The onshore Indian investor operates within the Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS) 
framework. Depending on their circumstances, this can offer meaningful or constrained access 
to global markets. Fund -of-funds and international exchange -traded funds (ETFs)  can also 
offer alternative access routes, as opposed to direct securities alone.  
For the offshore Indian investor, the challenge is less about access to India than choosing the 
most appropriate investment route. They can generally invest through onshore accounts, while 
offshore-listed funds and ETFs may offer a more convenient alternat ive. The preferred 
structure will depend on their residency, tax domicile and product eligibility, making careful 
planning important even where access itself is relatively broad. 
As an example, India’s Gujarat International Finance Tec -City (GIFT City), home to the 
country’s International Financial Services Centre (IFSC), is one potential implementation route 
available to both onshore and offshore Indian investors. For offshore investors, GIFT City offers 
an efficient way to access India-linked and global investment products with little or no need for 
INR conversion, while allowing foreign -currency repatriation. For onshore investors, it can 
facilitate USD -denominated exposure throu gh foreign -currency accounts under the LRS, 
providing a domestically regulated, yet globally oriented platform for portfolio diversification.

Portfolio risk –  
concentration on 
one side, under-
allocation on the 
other 
 The onshore Indian investor’s core risk is concentration and complacency: a portfolio almost 
entirely exposed to India’s domestic economic cycle, policy environment and sectoral 
composition. This is not a prediction that these will deteriorate, as India’s structural growth story 
remains compelling. Rather, this implies the risk is structural when all equity exposure sits 
within a single market, causing the portfolio to react simultaneously to the same domestic 
catalysts. By contrast, the offshore Indian investor faces the opposite problem: under-allocation 
to one of the world’s fastest-growing economies, driven partly by access frictions and partly by 
the difficulty of sizing a home-market allocation through a global investment framework.

Fig. 1  
Risk profile breakdown between an Indian onshore investor vs a USD offshore investor

INR onshore investor USD offshore investor

Starting point  India-based investor whose wealth, spending and 
returns are primarily measured in INR 
NRI whose portfolio benchmark is typically USD or 
another major currency 
Currency lens  Foreign assets add currency risk, but can also 
hedge INR depreciation 
Indian asset exposure adds INR risk; local returns 
can be reduced or bolstered when translated back 
to USD 
Access route  Typically through LRS or family office structures  Typically use ETFs, Foreign Portfolio Investor (FPI) 
structures or the Fully Accessible Route (FAR) 
Portfolio role   Indian assets remain the core; global assets 
diversify sector, cycle and currency exposure 
Global markets remain the core; India is a 
deliberate growth and real-yield overweight 
Core risk  Home-market concentration, domestic-cycle 
dependence and limited sector breadth 
Under-allocation to India, INR volatility and 
operational complexity

Source: Standard Chartered

### Page 4

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  4

A diversified portfolio makes sense 
regardless of the investor profile 
Before examining the specific assets available to Global 
Indian investors, it is useful to step back and ask a more 
fundamental question: why hold multiple asset classes at all? 
The simple answer is diversification. Because different asset 
classes play dif ferent roles and perform differently across 
economic and market conditions, combining them thoughtfully 
can reduce risk, create more consistent outcomes and build 
greater portfolio resilience than relying on any single asset 
class alone. 
Each major asset class tends to perform best under different 
economic conditions. Equities usually benefit when corporate 
earnings are growing and investor confidence is strong, but 
they can fall sharply when growth disappoints or credit 
conditions tighten. Bonds can provide income and may hold 
their value, or even appreciate, when growth slows and 
central banks ease policy, but they typically face pressure 
when inflation is high and interest rates rise. Gold has long 
been viewed as a geopolitical and infla tion hedge, and its 
relatively low correlation with equities and bonds can make it 
a useful portfolio diversifier. 
In practice, a portfolio concentrated in a single asset class – 
whether Indian equities, USD cash or any other standalone 
exposure – is implicitly relying on one set of economic and 
market conditions remaining favourable. A thoughtfully 
constructed multi-asset portfolio does not need to be the best 
performer in every year, but it is also unlikely to be the worst. 
Its purpose is to build resilience across market cycles: 
capturing growth when conditions are supportive, limiting 
downside when stress emerges, and compounding steadily 
over time in a way that no single asset class can consistently 
achieve on its own.

A practical asset-class toolkit for Global 
Indian investors 
Developed Market (DM) equities 
These are typically shares in large, established companies 
across the US, Europe, the UK and Japan and usually 
represent the largest and most liquid equity markets in the 
world. They are home to the technology giants, global 
healthcare leaders and consumer  brands that dominate their 
sectors globally – many of which have no equivalent in Indian 
indices. DM equities provide the broadest possible access to 
global corporate earnings growth, and for onshore investors, 
they provide USD and other major currency ex posure that 
structurally helps hedge against INR depreciation. For 
offshore investors, DM equities are the natural portfolio core, 
given their base currency and broad exposure of the asset 
class in global markets. 
Emerging Markets (EM) equities  
EM equities span a broad universe of developing economies, 
including China, South Korea, Taiwan, Brazil and other major 
EM markets. They offer greater growth potential than DM 
equities, but also tend to be more volatile. Their returns are 
supported by risi ng consumption, commodity cycles and the 
expansion of industry and the middle class. For Indian 
investors, EM equities provide exposure to regional peers that 
behave differently from Indian domestic markets, adding a 
layer of diversification within the equity sleeve.

1 The building blocks of a Global 
Indian portfolio

### Page 5

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  5

Indian equities  
Indian listed equities can be split up into two distinct sub-asset 
classes: 1) small - and mid -cap equities and 2) large -cap 
equities. The latter represent India’s established blue -chip 
companies with strong balance sheets and have relatively 
lower volatility within the Indian equity spectrum. These are 
typically the anchor of any Indian equity allocation. 
Meanwhile, small - and mid -cap equities tap into a different 
part of India’s growth story: the domestic consumption engine, 
the formalisation of the inform al economy and the 
entrepreneurial mid -market that scales rapidly in a high -
growth environment. They offer meaningfully higher potential 
returns, but also comes with higher volatility and lower 
liquidity, making the appropriate allocation dependent on the 
investor’s risk tolerance and investment time horizon.  
DM Investment Grade bonds 
Developed market investment grade bonds encompass two 
closely related asset classes that play a core defensive role in 
portfolios. Government bonds, such as US Treasuries, 
German Bunds, and UK Gilts, are issued by highly rated 
sovereigns and are widely regarded as the closest equivalent 
to a risk -free asset within global markets. Investment grade 
corporate bonds, issued by financially strong companies rated 
BBB- or above, offer higher yields than government bonds in 
return for accepting a modest amount of credit risk. 
Together, these assets provide an important source of portfolio 
stability. Government bonds have historically performed well 
during periods of economic uncertainty and market turbulence, 
helping to cushion equity losses when investors seek safer 
assets. In vestment grade corporate bonds enhance overall 
income generation while maintaining relatively low volatility, 
allowing investors to improve return potential without taking on 
the higher risks typically associated with equities. 
EM USD bonds 
EM USD bonds are USD -denominated debt issued by EM 
sovereign and quasi-sovereign borrowers, including countries 
such as Indonesia, Mexico, Brazil and South Africa. Their 
USD denomination removes direct exposure to the issuers’ 
local currencies, making them  a more accessible entry point 
into EM fixed income than local -currency bonds, particularly 
for offshore investors with hard -currency liabilities. For 
onshore investors, EM USD bonds add hard -currency fixed-
income exposure and can diversify portfolios conc entrated in 
Indian equities and INR interest -rate risk. However, their 
returns still depend on global interest rates, credit spreads and 
investor risk appetite. 
India government bonds  
The Indian bond market offers something that is increasingly 
rare globally: a genuine positive real (net -of-inflation) yield. 
India’s 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid-
2026 against a headline inflation of about 4.4%.  This 
represents a positive real yield of nearly 2.8% that compares 
favourably to most DM alternatives. India’s inclusion in global 
bond indices is now also channelling systematic passive 
inflows into this market, providing an additional structural 
demand tailwind.  
Gold 
Gold occupies a unique role in this framework particularly for 
Indian investors. Beyond its universal function as an inflation 
and geopolitical hedge, gold in INR terms has historically 
delivered strong returns during periods of INR weakness 
because gold prices are globally denominated in USD. When 
the INR depreciates, the INR value of gold rises, providing a 
natural cushion against the same currency dynamics that 
erode domestic purchasing power.

### Page 6

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  6

The case for global exposure for onshore Indian investors  – Most resident 
Indian investors already diversify across asset classes, typically holding a mix of 
equities, fixed income and, in some cases, commodities such as gold. However, 
this diversification is usually implemented through domestic assets. The gap, 
therefore, is not necessarily a lack of asset-class breadth, but a lack of geographic 
diversification. This is where global investing can play an important role: by adding 
exposure to different economies, currencies, sectors and market cycles  
beyond India.  
Argument

Since India’s market liberalisation in the early 1990s, the INR has depreciated significantly 
against the USD, weakening from approximately INR 31 per USD in 1994 to more than INR 95 
per USD by August 2026 – an annualised depreciation rate of 3.4%. This long-term depreciation 
is not simply a market fluctuation; it reflects a structural feature of an economy that has 
persistently run higher inflation than the US. For investors holding only INR-denominated assets, 
this creates a gradual erosion of global purchasing power over time. USD-denominated foreign 
assets can, therefore, serve as a structural currency hedge, helping preserve international 
purchasing power rather than representing a speculative currency bet. 
The INR 
depreciates 
structurally

### Page 7

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  7

Argument

Indian equity indices remain heavily weighted towards the financial, consumer discretionary and 
industrial sectors. By contrast, technology – the largest sector in global DM indices – has a much 
smaller representation in India’s listed market, despite the scale and importance of India’s 
broader technology economy. This gap has become more significant with the rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI). While India has strong technology talent and growing AI adoption, Indian 
markets offer little -to-no exposure to technology in the investible equity market. As a result, 
investors with portfolios concentrated in Indian equities have limited exposure to global 
technology leaders. Adding DM equities, therefore, provides sector diversification, including 
greater partici pation in the growth opportunities associated with AI, as well as geographic 
diversification.

### Page 8

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  8

Argument

Indian equities respond sharply to domestic catalysts: RBI rate decisions, monsoon outcomes, 
fiscal borrowing announcements and India -specific political developments. When these factors 
turn negative, they tend to affect domestic equity positions simultane ously. Global equity 
markets are not driven by the same catalysts, so a domestic Indian growth slowdown does not 
cause US corporate earnings to fall or other global equities to retreat. Adding a global equity 
sleeve means that not all equity exposure moves in tandem during a domestic downturn.  
The same logic applies to bonds. Indian bonds respond primarily to RBI monetary policy, 
domestic inflation and India’s fiscal borrowing calendar. These drivers are largely independent 
of the global rate cycle. An investor holding only Indian equities and Indian bonds has all of their 
assets exposed to the same set of domestic macro factors at once. A portfolio that blends Indian 
and global assets reduces this localised cycle dependency, spreading exposure across markets 
that follow different cycles. 
Market cycles are 
not synchronised

### Page 9

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  9

The case for Indian asset exposure for offshore Indian investors  – India 
stands out as one of the few major economies where 6 -7% annual real GDP 
growth remains a credible base case through the end of the decade. For offshore 
investors, the allocation case is not built on growth alone, but on four reinforcing 
pillars: a structural growth premium unmatched among large economies; reforms 
that have improved the quality and breadth of that growth; a demographic 
dividend that still has years to run; and a digital public infrastructure revolution 
that is lifting productivity at population scale.

The growth premium  
According to the International Monetary Fund, India remains the world’s fastest-growing major economy. Real 
GDP expanded by 7.6% in 2025 and is projected to grow by 6.5% in 2026 – outpacing China at 4.4%, G7 
economies at 1.6%, and EM and developing economies at 3.9%. This is not a one-year outlier. Apart from the 
Covid-19 pandemic shock, India has sustained a 6 -8% real GDP growth range for much of the past decade, 
reinforcing its structural growth premium. 
This structural growth is also reflected in equity -market performance. Since 2001, Indian equities have 
delivered annualised returns of 10 .1% in USD terms , outperforming DM equities, which returned 7.5% 
annualised over the same period. This long-term track record reinforces the case for including Indian equities 
as a deliberate source of growth within an offshore investor’s portfolio. 
For offshore investors, there is a clear benchmark gap. A global equity benchmark allocation of around 1.3% 
to India materially understates the country’s economic weight. India accounts for roughly 8.2% of global GDP 
on a purchasing power parity basis in 2025, which is significantly higher than its equity index weight. As Indian 
capital markets deepen and foreign ownership constraints ease, this gap between economic weight and index 
representation should continue to narrow, while rising benchmark inclusion can create a recurring source of 
passive inflows into Indian assets.

Economic and market reforms 
India's growth story over the past decade has not just been about the pace of GDP expansion. It also reflects 
a series of market -oriented reforms that have strengthened the investment environment and made growth 
more durable and accessible to long -term cap ital. Examples include the Goods and Services Tax, the 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, the Real Estate Regulation and Development Act and measures to deepen 
and formalise domestic capital markets. Taken together, these reforms have improved the structural 
competitiveness of the Indian economy in ways that show up in productivity growth, foreign direct investment 
inflows and market depth – not just in headline GDP numbers.

1 Why Indian assets still belong in every 
offshore Indian investor portfolio

### Page 10

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  10

Demographic dividend  
India’s demographic advantage is a long -term structural tailwind, not a short -term population bulge. The 
country has the world’s largest working -age population and, with a median age of around 29.2 (according to 
Worldometer’s latest data), remains one of the youngest major economies. As more Indians enter the formal 
workforce, earn higher incomes and build financial savings, they can support a broader domestic consumption 
base, deeper financial intermediation and continued cap ital-market growth. The shift in household savings 
from physical assets, such as gold and real estate, towards financial assets reinforces this trend. Monthly 
systematic investment plan (SIP) inflows into mutual funds, which have risen from approximately INR 36.6bn 
in 2016 to more than IN R 310bn in 2026, are a clear expression of this demographic and savings transition. 
They also create a durable, domestically -sourced demand base for Indian equities that is less dependent on 
foreign capital flows. 
However, the demographic dividend is not automatic; its realisation will depend on equipping a growing 
workforce with employable, industry -relevant skills and creating sufficient productive jobs. India has made 
meaningful investments in this direction thro ugh the Skill India Mission, including vocational training, 
apprenticeships and courses aligned with emerging sectors. Sustained improvements in training quality, 
employer alignment and job creation will, therefore, be critical to convert India’s demographic scale into lasting 
gains in productivity, income and economic growth.

Digital infrastructure 
India's digital public infrastructure – collectively known as the India Stack – has built a low-cost, inter-operable 
rails system that is accelerating formalisation, financial inclusion and commercial productivity at a speed with 
few global parallels. Exam ples include Aadhaar for identity, the Unified Payments Interface for payments, 
DigiLocker for documentation and the Open Network for Digital Commerce. Together, these platforms are 
productivity infrastructure: reducing the cost of doing business, formalising the informal economy and 
expanding the addressable market for financial services companies, fintechs, consumer platforms and logistics 
businesses in ways that compound into earnings growth over time.

The India fixed income opportunity 
India's 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid -2026, against headline consumer inflation of 
approximately 4.4% – implying a real yield of around 2.4%. This compares to near-zero or negative real yields 
in much of the developed world over the past decade and to real yields of 1-2% available in most comparable 
EM bond markets.

For onshore Indian investors, adding global assets can improve portfolio 
resilience without diluting expected INR returns. Our modelling shows that moving 
from a fully domestic portfolio to a 15% global allocation lifts the expected return 
only marginally,  from 8.42% to 8.44%, but reduces expected volatility more 
meaningfully, from 18.38% to 16.86%. This improves the risk-adjusted return from 
0.46 to 0.50. The benefit comes from exposure to markets, sectors and currencies 
that do not move in lockstep with I ndian assets, helping reduce domestic 
concentration risk while adding access to different structural growth drivers.

### Page 12

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  12

For offshore investors with globally diversified portfolios, Indian assets are best viewed as a deliberate 
strategic tilt towards a differentiated source of long -term growth, rather than as a near -term portfolio 
efficiency trade. Based on our forward -looking Capital Market Assumptions (CMAs), increasing the 
allocation to Indian assets to 15% leaves the expected USD return broadly unchanged at 6.10%, while 
expected volatility rises from 10.32% to 11.00%, reflecting the additional EM and currency risks.  
However, these aggregate estimates may not fully capture the opportunity in Indian financial market assets. 
Our CMAs show that the case for Indian equities is compelling when viewed from a returns perspective 
alone. 
The challenge to portfolio efficiency arises from Indian equities’ relatively high expected volatility. For 
context, our CMAs suggest an expected volatility of 29% for Indian large -cap equities. This number is 
considerably higher than the level experienced over the last 10 years (18.9%) and modestly higher than the 
level over the past several decades. This is a key point because the drag on risk-adjusted returns in Fig. 8 
comes from the higher expected volatility of Indian equities rather than any challenge to the expected return. 
As an example, Fig. 9 illustrates that calibrating Indian equity volatility to more recent market experience 
produces a more balanced assessment of portfolio risk and return. Using recent history as an example 
shows that risk-adjusted returns actually improve modestly from 0.79 to 0.81.  
Overall, this means the debate is less about how high a return Indian equities are expected to deliver over 
the coming years. Instead, how volatile Indian assets are likely to be is key to whether adding them to global 
portfolios improves risk-adjusted returns.

### Page 13

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026 13
Pulling this together, we would argue that a strong case exists for a measured allocation to Indian assets –
particularly equities – exists, in our view, provided two conditions are met.
First, investors should retain conviction that the structural drivers of India’s return premium – including
earnings growth, improving governance and an expanding investable universe – remain intact. This is key

to being comfortable with the expected return of Indian equities and bonds.
Second, investors must be willing and able to tolerate the volatility associated with Indian equities. While
one could argue that the assumed volatility in our CMAs is high relative to the level experienced in recent
decades, under most scenarios, it would remain meaningfully higher than that of DM equities.
Taken together, offshore Indian investors should consider incorporating Indian assets within a globally

diversified portfolio. However, the allocation should be deliberate and sized in line with their objectives and
risk tolerance, reflecting a conscious investment view and not just the familiarity of a home bias.

CMAs.
These results show that cross-border diversification serves different needs for different investors but leads
to the same portfolio conclusion. For onshore Indian investors, global assets can help lower volatility,

broaden sector exposure and provide a structural hedge against INR depreciation. For offshore Indian
investors, India offers a deliberate growth and real -yield allocation that is under -represented in global
benchmarks. In both cases, combining Indian and global assets can build a more resil ient portfolio across
market cycles.

### Page 14

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  14

Resident onshore Indian investors and offshore Indian investors begin from opposite starting points. One 
is anchored in India and looking outward; the other is anchored globally and looking inward. Their regulatory 
regimes, base currencies and available instruments differ. Yet the portfolio destination is the same: 
meaningful exposure to both India and the world. 
That conclusion reflects the reality of an interconnected investment world. Indian corporate earnings are 
shaped by global commodity prices, technology supply chains and demand from DMs. At the same time, 
companies in the US, Europe and Japan are exposed to the consumption growth of emerging economies, 
including India. Bond markets may respond first to domestic central banks, but they are still influenced by 
global rate cycles; gold is priced in USD, yet its impact is felt across currencies. No investor can  afford to 
treat the other half of the world as irrelevant. 
What differs is not the principle, but the path. The onshore Indian investor adds global exposure as a 
deliberate extension of an India -centric portfolio, seeking additional return opportunities while gaining a 
structural hedge against INR depreciation. Th e offshore investor builds India exposure as a deliberate 
overweight above global benchmarks, accepting INR volatility as the cost of accessing India’s growth 
premium and real-yield opportunity. The appropriate allocation will differ by profile and risk appetite, but the 
core message is the same: both investors need both markets.

destination

## Document 432: What sector analysis tells us about Europe's recovery, two years after the _Draghi_ report (2026-10-12)

WHAT SECTOR ANALYSIS TELLS US ABOUT EUROPE'S RECOVERY, TWO YEARS AFTER THE "DRAGHI" REPORT
1 That amounts to EUR 800 billion to be committed in the EU by the end of the decade, which would bring defence budgets to 3.5% of GDP , up from 2.4% of GDP in
2026 (estimated figure). See Colliac, S. (2026), “Readiness 2030: One Year After Its Launch, the European Rearmament Plan Is on Track,” BNP Paribas EcoWeek, March.

2 Since 2025, critical metals have also been the focus of “strategic projects” for extraction in Europe, with the European Commission having just launched an ambi-
tious second wave, targeting lithium and cobalt in particular. See: Commission Selects 46 New Strategic Projects to Bolster the EU’s Supply of Critical Raw Materials
3 See Derrien G. (2026), “Next Generation EU: The Program Is Nearing Its End, but Its Impact on Economic Activity Will Endure,” BNP Paribas Chart of the Week, Sep-

tember 23.
4 NextGenerationEU Shows Strong Delivery Record as Implementation Reaches the Finish Line, October 7, 2026.
In 2026, the European economy will not perform better than it did in 2025. At 1.4% (IMF estimate), its growth remains half
as fast as in the United States. Still, there are more and more signs that Europe is regaining strength. The energy crisis,
which has now been ongoing for eight months, would have caused it to halt in other times. Beneath an apparent sluggish

activity, productivity is rebounding, while many sectors are picking up steam.
Insufficient growth and innovation, a technological lag, and an ex -
cessive dependence on China and the United States: two years ago,
the “Draghi” report painted a troubling picture of Europe and laid the
groundwork for a massive effort to revive the economy. Almost im -
mediately afterwards, the European Union (EU) adopted a “competi -
tiveness compass,” with the primary aim of strengthening its strategic

autonomy.
A 3D OUTLOOK: DEFENCE, DIGITALISATION, DECARBONISATION
Unsurprisingly given the geopolitical context, the most significant ini -
tiatives focused on defence: since March 2025, the SAFE (Security Ac -
tion for Europe) instrument has enabled the Commission to raise funds
on the markets (up to EUR 150 billion) to support member states in
implementing the “Readiness 2030” rearmament programme 1. Here,
the planning stage has long been surpassed, and disbursements for

the acquisition of military equipment are accelerating. According to the
EDA (European Defense Agency), European defence spending is projec-
ted to reach EUR 454 billion in 2026, already 30% more than in 2024.
The EU-27 have also set out to catch up in the field of artificial intel -
ligence (AI), committing up to EUR 200 billion in investments through
public-private partnerships. From fostering a culture of AI to promoting
and retaining talents, supporting startups, and sharing experiences and

data, the initiative is designed to be comprehensive. Its cornerstone is a
dedicated EUR 20 billion fund, co-financed by the Commission and the
European Investment Bank, which aims to establish five “giga-facto -
ries” across Europe capable of hosting and training the most powerful
AI models.
The EU is also leading the counteroffensive on other fronts, such as the
supply of critical metals, which it is seeking to secure through trade
agreements (most recently with Australia and South Africa) 2, and the

accelerated decarbonisation of the economy (with specific support pro-
vided to energy-intensive sectors). Its “Clean Industry Pact” is expected
to receive up to EUR 100 billion in funding from the Innovation Fund or
the European Union Emissions Trading System.
BENEATH THE SURFACE WINDS, SUPPORTIVE CURRENTS
In a report dated June 2026, the Institut Montaigne estimated that
nearly one-third of the recommendations in the “Draghi” report had
been implemented. Even before taking up the report, the EU had

already launched a significant investment effort through its NextGe -
nerationEU (NGEU) program: EUR 573 billion distributed via the
Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) between 2020 and 2026, with a
focus on energy infrastructure (particularly renewables), railway, di -
gital & connectivity 3. According to the Commission, two-thirds of the
6,000 “milestones and targets” assigned to governments have already
been linked to the RRF4.

for a changing
world
What are the results on the ground? An analysis based solely on GDP
trends would not be conclusive: after a rebound in 2025, European
growth is projected to slow slightly in 2026. The International Mone -
tary Fund (IMF) estimates it at 1.4%, a rate nearly half that expected
in the United States.
In fact, the EU is showing remarkable resilience in the face of another

major energy shock that, in other times, might have pushed it into
recession. The German economy, one of the most exposed, is even gai-
ning momentum despite headwinds, a sign that, beneath the surface,
certain trends are gaining traction.
To assess the conflicting forces that have been shaping the Euro -
pean economy since the publication of the “Draghi” report, a sectoral
breakdown may prove useful. In particular, it allows to quantify the
ongoing transformation of the automotive industry, where a 150-year-

old technology — the internal combustion engine — is gradually giving
way to electric power.
TRANSFORMATION OF THE AUTOMOTIVE SECTOR
Year in and year out, European automakers are continuing their trans-
formation. After a lull in 2023-24 due to a decline in government subsi-
dies, European production of electric vehicles is picking up again (+30%
in 2025). In 2026, based on figures from the battery sector (production
up 25% y/y in July), this momentum continues. However, the volumes

involved (3.2 million electric vehicles assembled annually in Europe
compared to nearly 17 million in China, representing 75% of the global
market5) remain too low to offset the decline in sales in the traditional
internal combustion engine sector.
Compared to its pre-COVID average (2015-19), total European automo-
bile production has thus fallen by 30% in volume — a historic slump.
In 2026, against a backdrop of soaring fuel prices, the trend remains

negative (down 9.6% y/y in July, according to Eurostat). Upstream in the
supply chain, automotive suppliers, machine tool manufacturers, and
metal manufacturers are struggling (see Chart 1).
THE RISE OF AI
As an example of “creative destruction,” artificial intelligence is beco -
ming, alongside the energy transition, a driving force for the European
economy, as evidenced by the boom in related industries. In Germany,
the difficulties of the automotive sector stand in stark contrast to the

strong momentum in orders for electrical and electronic equipment.
In Europe, the production of IT equipment (computers, peripherals,
etc.) is growing at a rate of 40% per year — a level not seen since the
rapid digitalisation that accompanied the 2020-21 health crisis. Ge -
nerally speaking, most sectors involved in generating or transmitting
electricity (generators, transformers, cables, etc.) are currently outper-
forming the average. This is also true of the rail industry (locomotives

and other rolling stock), driven by “green” investment efforts under -
taken as part of the NGEU program.
5 Data from 2025. Source: IEA (International Energy Agency), Global EV Outlook, 2026.
6 See Derrien, G. (2026), ”When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments,” BNP Paribas EcoWeek, October 5.
DEFENCE EFFORTS
If there is one area where actions finally match commitments, it is
defence. Fighting vehicles, weapons and ammunition, as well as te -

lecommunications and optical equipment — which are ubiquitous in
drone systems — are all sectors where production volumes are brea -
king records and growth rates range from 20% to 50% annually.
***
Far from the image of stagnation it often projects, the EU has, for se -
veral years now, been making a significant effort to modernise and
strengthen the foundations of its economy. Although close to its end,
the NGEU programme will continue to support investment, while ini -

tiatives taken in the wake of the “Draghi” report are generating further
momentum. Sectoral analysis also shows that Europe is catching up
with the AI wave — admittedly belatedly, but with increasing momen-
tum.
Whether by coincidence or as a result, its hourly productivity has re -
bounded significantly over the past few quarters, returning to the 1%
annual growth rate it had before the pandemic (see Chart 2). Finally,
contrary to a fairly widespread belief, European companies have lar -

gely managed to maintain their competitiveness. Aside from the very
specific case of China, their trade in manufactured goods outside the
EU has tended to strengthen in recent years6.
Jean-Luc Proutat
jean-luc.proutat@bnpparibas.com

## Document 433: What Will Warsh Do_

Source PDF: all report pdf/What Will Warsh Do_.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: What Will Warsh Do?
Date:    5 hours ago
What Will Warsh Do?

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
What Will Warsh Do?
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 20 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Olav Ahrens Røtne / Unsplash

I. The Fed: To Hike or Not To Hike?
The Federal Reserve announces its next interest-rate decision on
Wednesday, October 28. According to CME FedWatch, the federal funds
futures market assigns an 84% probability to a pause. The market expects
a 25bps hike in December and roughly 50bps of tightening over the next
six months (chart).

### Page 2

The two-year Treasury yield remains roughly 90bps above the federal
funds rate, consistent with expectations for further tightening (chart).
Why are markets pricing in such a low probability of an October hike
despite September's hot CPI and PPI reports? The explanation may lie
more in Fed commentary than in the economic data:
(1) Fed officials favor patience. New York Fed President John Williams
and Vice Chair Philip Jefferson, who alongside Chair Kevin Warsh form

the influential troika, have signaled little urgency to hike in October.
Governor Christopher Waller suggested that hikes need not occur at
consecutive meetings, while Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle Bowman
also favors patience. Together, their forward guidance has steered the
markets toward pricing in an October pause. Even Warsh's refusal to

### Page 3

provide guidance at the IMF-World Bank Annual Meetings in Bangkok on
October 16 was interpreted as supportive of a pause.
(2) Warsh's three problems. The markets remain heavily influenced by
the very forward guidance that Warsh wants to abandon. This creates
three challenges: First, other Fed officials have continued to provide
guidance despite Warsh’s preference to move away from it. Second, that
guidance influences bond yields, making it harder for Warsh to get a clean

signal from the bond market. Third, that risks boxing him into policy
decisions that he may oppose, as he could be reluctant to surprise the
markets.
(3) The solution. We suspect Warsh would prefer an October hike.
Following the FOMC's September 16 decision to raise the federal funds
rate by 25bps, Warsh said he would be “hard pressed to describe broad
financial conditions as restrictive,” characterizing the hike as removing a
dose of accommodation.

Raising rates in October, even if the markets aren't fully pricing in such a
move, could help Warsh address his three problems. It would signal that
forward guidance from other Fed officials, even influential members of the
traditional troika, may no longer carry the weight it once did. That could
encourage investors to place less weight on Fed commentary, giving
Warsh a clearer signal from the bond market about the appropriate path of
monetary policy. In turn, it would reduce the risk of getting boxed in by

market expectations shaped by forward guidance that Warsh does not
necessarily agree with.
(4) Our call. We estimate the neutral federal funds rate at 4.25%–4.50%
and believe the Fed should return to that range, reversing last year's three
25bps insurance cuts. Those cuts were intended to protect against labor-
market weakness (that we considered overblown) and were made at a
time when the Fed was too complacent about inflation.
We believe that Warsh should try to persuade his colleagues to hike next

week. Then again, he might not do so because the midterm elections are
too close.
II. US Economy

### Page 4

The latest US economic data suggest that monetary policy remains
accommodative, exerting little restraint on economic activity or inflation:
(1) Consumer spending. Redbook same-store sales rose 9.7% y/y
during the week of October 16. The four-week average climbed 9.1%, its
fastest pace since early July and well above the 2025 average of 5.8%
(chart).
(2) Labor market. The latest weekly ADP data show private-sector
employment rising by an average of 18,500 jobs per week over the four

weeks through October 3, implying roughly 74,000 jobs per month (chart).
This suggests that the labor market remains in good shape, supporting
continued brisk consumer spending.

### Page 5

(3) Import prices. September's data on import prices point to persistent
inflation pressures. The import price index surged 8.1% y/y, the fastest
pace since July 2022 (chart). The index excluding petroleum imports rose
6.0%.
Import prices from the newly industrialized Asian countries surged 12.5%
y/y in September, mostly reflecting booming AI-related demand for
semiconductors from South Korea and Taiwan (chart). That suggests the
AI buildout will remain inflationary for now, before AI brings the fruits of

disinflationary productivity growth.
(4) GDP. The Atlanta Fed's GDPNow model estimates Q3 real GDP
growth at 3.5% (saar). Real final sales to private domestic purchasers, a
key gauge of underlying demand, is projected to rise 4.7%, a pace last
matched in Q1-2023. Real consumer spending is expected to grow 3.8%,

### Page 6

clause.
